This is the formal account closest to our revertible effects: both pair each effect with the means to undo it rather than discharging it through a handler. The two differ in where reversibility resides, and in how much of it they demand. Heunen et al. work in a denotational, categorical setting where reversibility is a global property, guaranteed by construction since every computation is invertible, and the inverse is two-sided and recovered from the categorical structure. Cordis tracks inverses at runtime and requires less of them: not that the whole computation be reversible, but that each atomic effect admit a one-sided inverse, supplied by the caller at the point of application rather than derived, from which the inverse of any composite follows by composition (Section 3.1).

Graded types as unified effects and coeffects. Orchard et al. [88] proposed graded modal types as an umbrella notion encompassing both effect reasoning (via graded monads) and coeffect reasoning (via graded comonads), realized in the Granule language, demonstrating that a single type system can track both what a computation does and what it needs; more recent work extends coeffects to imperative Java-like languages [89, 90] and to call-by-push-value [91]. All of these operate at the type level: effects and coeffects are static annotations checked at compile time over lexically fixed scopes. Our contribution is orthogonal to this analysis: we lift the same two notions to runtime mechanisms, which lets Cordis handle dynamic composition. Temporal retraction and spatial dependency are re-resolved as the set of loaded components evolves, instead of being settled once over a fixed program text.

7.2 Programming Paradigms

Section 3.3.3 established the context paradigm as a discipline that mediates effects and coeffects through an explicit context. Two established paradigms warrant explicit comparison: one shares our terminology, the other our treatment of crosscutting concerns.

Context-oriented programming. COP [92, 93] equips a language with layers—partial method and class definitions that are activated and deactivated at runtime according to the execution context, so that behavior adapts without the base code naming its context dependencies [94]. COP and Cordis coincide in treating context as a first-class, runtime-mutable entity and in activating and deactivating behavior dynamically, but the resemblance is nominal. In COP, “context” denotes the ambient execution situation (e.g., location, user, mode), and activation changes method dispatch within a dynamically scoped extent; a layer neither tracks the side effects it induces nor reverts them, and activation is not governed by dependency satisfaction. In Cordis, the context is the Γ∞ entity mediating effects and coeffects: activation runs a component’s revertible effects and is driven by reactive coeffect satisfaction (Section 3.2), and deactivation reverts them in full. COP varies what behavior runs; Cordis composes and reverts what effects and dependencies a component installs. Their difference is one of trade-off. COP folds activation into the host language’s method dispatch, gaining dynamically-scoped layer extents at the cost of language specificity, whereas Cordis, as a language-agnostic overlay, resolves activation reactively over a shared context. Cordis can thus express as a coeffect only COP’s global, value-driven fragment: context-dependent selection among implementations, but not dynamically-scoped activation.

Aspect-oriented programming. AOP [95, 96] modularizes a crosscutting concern into an aspect: a pointcut that quantifies over join points selected in the base program, and advice woven in at each. Cordis addresses the same problem of contextual behavior that would otherwise scatter across components, but its analogue of an aspect is a coeffect: a shared point of mediation many components declare a dependence on, so that crosscutting behavior can be reshaped there without editing any of them. The two paradigms then differ on two axes. (1) Declaration versus obliviousness: an AOP pointcut is oblivious and quantified, matching arbitrary join points whose code is unaware it is advised, whereas Cordis confines crosscutting to the coeffects each component declares, so its reach is exactly that declared surface. This yields determinacy and traceability: an application orchestrator can inspect and govern what cross-cuts a component at the configuration layer, without reading or analyzing its source, whereas an AOP concern is legible only through the aspects that quantify over it. (2) Lifecycle integration: a crosscutting change in Cordis is carried by a component’s effects, reverted when the component unloads and propagated reactively to its dependents, so it is one move within the dynamic composition model; dynamic-AOP systems [97, 98] can also weave and unweave at runtime, but as a standalone operation, neither bound to a component’s lifecycle nor triggering re-resolution among the advised code.

7.3 Temporal Composability

Temporal composability concerns replacing or removing a component in a running program while recovering the effects it installed. Prior approaches divide by how they treat a departing component’s state and effects: carrying state forward to a successor version, recovering effects through developer-authored cleanup, reversing effects automatically within a scope fixed in advance, or reclaiming resources from a record the runtime accumulates by interposing on an interface.

Stateful forward migration. A broad family of systems replaces components in a running program without downtime by carrying their state forward across versions. All observe the same timing discipline: a component may be swapped only once it reaches a safe, interaction-free point. Kramer and Magee established this criterion as quiescence [51], which Vandewoude et al. later relaxed to the less disruptive tranquility [52]; our rolling-update pattern (Section 6.2) enforces it by draining in-flight requests before unloading a provider. Dynamic software updating (DSU) then migrates state forward through hand-written transformation functions: Hicks et al.’s general-purpose DSU for C [99], Stoyle et al.’s type-safe update points via con-freeness analysis [100], and Hayden et al.’s Kitsune [101] all map old-version data to new-version representations, inheriting heap objects, open files, and connections in place while re-initializing whatever is left unmigrated. The same discipline extends to persistent state: Overeem et al. [102] convert a running event store’s data between schema versions through hand-written upgrade operations while keeping the system available. Erlang/OTP [15] takes the same stance at the process level, migrating state through code_change/3 and recovering from faults by restarting supervised processes rather than reverting their effects; JavaScript’s Hot Module Replacement (e.g., webpack [46], Vite [47]) does the same at the module level, handing state forward through the module.hot or import.meta.hot API across a reload. Compared with Cordis’s module replacement (Section 5.2), these approaches migrate in-memory state more gracefully: Cordis reverts the old component’s tracked effects and reapplies the new component’s from a clean slate, so a component’s own in-memory state does not survive a reload unless placed in a longer-lived dependency, and layering DSU-style forward migration atop revertible effects is future work. Cordis’s approach is nonetheless more general in two respects: it needs no hand-written migration functions of the kind DSU and HMR require, and it supports unloading a component entirely and recovering its resources, not merely updating one in place.

Developer-authored recovery. A second family recovers a component’s effects through cleanup or compensation logic that the developer writes by hand. Plugin lifecycle conventions (e.g., OSGi [50], Eclipse’s extension points, IntelliJ and VSCode) delegate cleanup to developer-written unload callbacks; the Command pattern [103] encapsulates an operation together with an undo method for undo/redo stacks; the saga model [49] structures a long-lived transaction as steps each paired with a compensating action; algebraic effect handlers can attach finalizers that run on teardown [104]; and event sourcing [105] retracts state by appending compensating events rather than executing an inverse at all. In all of them the inverse is an unenforced duty, decoupled from the operation, so that a forgotten one leaks resources silently (as documented empirically in Section 1.2.1). React’s useEffect hook [106] comes closest to pairing an effect with its inverse structurally, returning a cleanup the runtime invokes before each re-execution and on unmount. Its shortfall is composability: a hook may be called only at the top level of a component or another hook, never inside a conditional, loop, or nested function, and its effect body accepts neither an async function nor an iterator. Effects thus cannot be assembled from other effects or interleaved with control flow, leaving nothing from which a composite inverse could be derived. Cordis effects carry no such restriction: they are ordinary operations that compose freely and may run asynchronously, and require a hand-written inverse only for each atomic effect, from which the inverse of any composite is derived by composition, so that assembling existing effects requires writing no inverses at all. This structural pairing of every effect with its inverse makes complete recovery an invariant of the system rather than a matter of developer discipline.

Statically scoped reversal. A third family reverses effects automatically, by construction, but confines reversal to a scope fixed in advance. Software transactional memory [107, 108], descended from hardware transactional memory [109], records a read/write log so that a group of memory operations either commits or aborts, rolling memory back to its pre-transaction state. Reversible computing, from Landauer and Bennett’s thermodynamic analyses [110, 111] to reversible languages such as Janus [112], goes further and makes every step of a whole computation globally invertible. Reversible process calculi build backtracking into the semantics itself: RCCS [113] carries a memory alongside each process and admits a step to be taken back when the past it leads to is causally equivalent, and Phillips and Ulidowski [114] derive reversible operators for CCS, ACP, and CSP uniformly while preserving their forward operational semantics. Their causal-consistency criterion is the concurrent counterpart of the order Cordis’s recovery follows, an accumulator applying a component’s own inverses in last-in-first-out order and the guard of Section 4.3.1 deferring a provider’s withdrawal until its consumers have deactivated (Theorem 63). The reach, however, is fixed by the semantics, every action performed remaining undoable, whereas a Cordis component supplies an inverse for each atomic effect and its accumulator brings the context back to where its composition began. Linear types [115], RAII [4], and Rust’s ownership system [61] tie a resource’s release to a lexical region. Each fixes the scope and reach of reversal statically; Cordis, by contrast, fixes no such scope in advance: it reverts arbitrary context operations over a component’s lifecycle, and treats lexical resource management as complementary, appropriate for local resources within a single component.

Interposed reclamation. A fourth family reclaims what a component acquired without the component itself supplying the inverses, by recording its acquisitions at an interface the runtime controls. Nooks [77] wraps every call crossing the boundary between the Linux kernel and its loadable extensions, so that the kernel objects an extension touches pass through an object tracker whose record tells the recovery manager what to release when the extension fails; shadow drivers [78] tap the same calls from the other side, recording the requests and configuration that determine a driver’s state so that a restarted instance can be restored to it. Akeso [116] obtains the record by compiler instrumentation instead, dividing kernel execution into nestable recovery domains that log their state changes and cross-thread dependencies, and rolling a faulting request back together with every domain that depends on it. Reclamation thus follows from a record the runtime maintains rather than from cleanup the developer remembers to write, which makes this family the closest systems-level precedent for revertible effects. It differs from Cordis in vocabulary and in reach. The platform fixes what can be recorded, whether as release code per kernel object type, one shadow per driver class, or an inverse per instrumented allocator, so a component may hold only resources the platform already knows how to release; a Cordis component instead introduces effects of its own and supplies an inverse for each atomic one (Section 3.1). Reclamation is likewise bounded by a request that commits or a restart of the same extension, whereas Cordis reverts over a component’s whole lifetime and propagates removal to its dependents, which release their own effects in turn (Section 3.2).

7.4 Spatial Composability

Spatial composability concerns how a component’s dependencies on others are declared and bound. Prior mechanisms divide by how binding responds to change: wiring dependencies once at initialization, reacting to the availability of whole components, or propagating change at the granularity of individual values.

Initialization-time dependency wiring. Two established mechanisms wire components together at initialization time. Dependency injection frameworks [38] (e.g., Spring [117], Guice, Angular, Inversify) inject dependencies into components at initialization, and UI framework context (e.g., Vue.js’s provide/inject and React’s Context API) passes them along a component tree. Some support dynamic scoping (e.g., Spring’s prototype/request scopes, Angular’s hierarchical injectors), but neither re-resolves reactively: when a provider is replaced or removed at runtime, existing dependents are neither deactivated nor re-initialized, and none offers lifecycle management of the kind our component state machine provides. Cordis’s reactive coeffects (Section 3.2) supply this: the notification mechanism triggers lifecycle transitions whenever the satisfaction predicate changes.

Availability-reactive component models. The closest precedent to our reactive coeffects reacts to service availability. OSGi’s Declarative Services and iPOJO [118, 119] let components declare provided and required services, with the runtime automatically activating and deactivating them as services appear and disappear; iPOJO’s Gravity project [119] explicitly targets autonomous runtime adaptation to changing service availability, and its provide/require model directly prefigures Cordis’s ctx.provide/ctx.get pattern. R-OSGi [53] extends the same abstraction transparently to distributed settings via RPC, mapping network failures to service-withdrawal events, a pattern Section 6.2 discusses as an extension of the Cordis model. All these systems recover through a deactivation callback, which is limited in two ways. First, the callback is hand-written, so resource safety rests on developer discipline and a forgotten one leaks silently. Second, the callback is synchronous: should teardown require an asynchronous exchange with the departing dependency, the frameworks offer no protocol to await it, forcing a blocking wait against a reference that may already be stale. Cordis’s reactive coeffects close both gaps: deactivation reverts the dependents’ accumulated effects, and its inertial Unloading state (Section 4.3.3) runs asynchronous teardown to completion before acting on further change.

Value-level reactivity. Functional reactive programming (FRP) [120] and its modern incarnations (e.g., signals [121, 122] in SolidJS, Vue’s reactivity system, Angular Signals) propagate change at a value-level granularity: when a signal changes, derived computations are re-evaluated synchronously or under a scheduler [123]. Cordis’s reactive coeffects act at a component-level granularity, adding asynchronous lifecycle semantics that value-level propagation does not model. The same granularity difference runs the other way for consistency: propagating in a turn, in an order the dependency graph fixes, lets FRP require that no derived computation read a mixture of updated and stale inputs, which is glitch freedom [124], whereas Cordis has no counterpart of a turn, orchestration actions arriving one at a time, and guarantees only that no single transition straddles two resolutions of its coeffects (Theorem 64). The two are complementary rather than competing: a Cordis coeffect can itself carry reactive values, and a component updates on only the parts it actually consumes, refining component-level reactivity into finer-grained reactive coeffects that span both levels.

8 Conclusion

We have presented a formal foundation for dynamic composability by lifting the classical concepts of effects and coeffects to runtime mechanisms. Revertible effects address local temporal composability: every context transformation carries an inverse that the runtime tracks, and both tracking and recovery preserve composition, so the context is recovered upon component removal. Reactive coeffects address local spatial composability: a component is notified against its coeffect specification whenever the context changes, each change classified as activating, deactivating, or neutral, with coeffect isolation varying what a declared key resolves to and coeffect interception varying how the binding is used. We unify the effect context and the coeffect context into a single context type, in which an observational equivalence on the coeffects supplies the effects with independence, constituting a programming paradigm for spatiotemporal composability. Combining these mechanisms into the notion of a component then gives a calculus of dynamic composition, whose metatheory carries spatiotemporal composability from a single component to a whole system of interleaved components. We realize this paradigm as the Cordis meta-framework, with a core library providing effect tracking and coeffect resolution, as well as a declarative component loader with configuration reconciliation and hot module replacement. The Koishi case study validates the design of Cordis in a production system with over 4000 community plugins.

Beyond human-curated plugin ecosystems, a compelling direction for future validation is self-evolving agent harnesses (Section 1.2.2), where an AI agent generates and replaces its own harness components continuously and with little human oversight. Applying Cordis in such a setting would validate the temporal guarantees of complete recovery under rapid component replacement, as well as the spatial guarantees of dependency coordination under frequent topological change. Such validation would demonstrate the paradigm’s applicability as a foundation for recoverable, coordinated, and continuous self-evolution in agent harnesses and other autonomous systems.

Implementation

The Ruby, traced to the paper

cordis.rb — the complete library, pure Ruby stdlib, contexts immutable so the theorems stay executable. The code below is generated from the committed source by build.rb; it cannot drift from what runs. Every dashed reference is a link into the paper above — oxblood for the temporal machinery, blue for the spatial. A floating button returns you to where you left off.

Temporal machinery — effects that remember how to leave

Three constructs carry all of §3.1: Effect (a function returning its own inverse), EffectContext (state paired with the accumulator), and Independence (the discipline that licenses out-of-order withdrawal).

module Cordis

cordis.rb · lines 1–19

1# frozen_string_literal: true
2
3# Cordis — a Ruby distillation of "A Programming Paradigm for Spatiotemporal
4# Composability" (Shi, Zhang & Cui; Peking University / DeepSeek-AI).
5#
6# The module is named after the paper's meta-framework (§1.3, contribution 5).
7# Every construct cites the Definition, Theorem, or Section it implements, so
8# code can be traced back to the paper — and the paper forward to running code.
9#
10# Contexts are immutable values (frozen); every effect is a pure function from
11# a context to a successor context paired with the inverse chosen at that state.
12# Purity is what makes the paper's theorems executable: see theorem_checks.rb.
13module Cordis
14  # id_Γ — the unit of composition (§3.1.1, monoid identity axiom).
15  IDENTITY = ->(gamma) { gamma }
16
17  # "A violated precondition is signalled as an error and produces no
18  # transition" (§3.2.1, remark after Definition 22).
19  class PreconditionViolation < StandardError; end

Cordis::Effect

The witnessed effect function: called at a state, returns the successor and the inverse chosen there. compose is the ⋄ operation — forwards chain, inverses accumulate reversed.

cordis.rb · lines 21–80

21  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
22  # Definition 8 — effect functions 𝔈_Γ and their witnessed refinement 𝔈*_Γ.
23  #
24  # An Effect maps a context γ to a pair (δ, g): the successor state and the
25  # inverse chosen *at γ*, at the point of application — not fixed a priori
26  # as in the track model of §3.1.1. The witness condition g(δ) = γ holds the
27  # inverse to reverting the effect exactly where it was applied.
28  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
29  class Effect
30    attr_reader :name
31
32    # The body is the effect function itself: γ → [δ, inverse].
33    def initialize(name = "effect", &body)
34      @name = name
35      @body = body
36      freeze
37    end
38
39    # e(γ) = (δ, g) — Definition 8.
40    def call(gamma)
41      @body.call(gamma)
42    end
43
44    # The forward map pr₁ ∘ e (Definition 17 names it a generator of 𝔐(e)).
45    def forward
46      effect = self
47      ->(gamma) { effect.call(gamma).first }
48    end
49
50    # Membership test for 𝔈*_Γ at γ: the inverse yielded at γ reverts to γ
51    # (the witness constraint g(δ) = γ of Definition 8).
52    def witnessed_at?(gamma)
53      delta, inverse = call(gamma)
54      inverse.call(delta) == gamma
55    end
56
57    # Definition 9 — effect composition f ⋄ g: g runs first, f second, and the
58    # inverses accumulate in the opposite order (s ∘ t): undo f, then undo g.
59    # Theorem 10: ⋄ is a monoid; Theorem 11: witnessing survives ⋄. Outcomes
60    # (eq. 22) are per-application and are not composed.
61    def compose(other)
62      f = self
63      Effect.new("#{f.name} ⋄ #{other.name}") do |gamma|
64        delta, s = other.call(gamma)
65        epsilon, t = f.call(delta)
66        [epsilon, ->(x) { s.call(t.call(x)) }]
67      end
68    end
69
70    # The monoid unit η_Γ := γ ↦ (γ, id_Γ) (Theorem 10.1).
71    def self.unit
72      Effect.new("η") { |gamma| [gamma, IDENTITY] }
73    end
74
75    # A pair (f, g) ∈ 𝔗_Γ induces the effect γ ↦ (f(γ), g) — one uniform
76    # inverse serving every state (Definition 8, discussion; Theorem 11.2).
77    def self.from_pair(name, forward, inverse)
78      Effect.new(name) { |gamma| [forward.call(gamma), inverse] }
79    end
80  end

Cordis::EffectContext

The pair (γ, φ). track composes an inverse onto the accumulator; apply is the lift — it also hands back the undo as a trackable effect, enabling selective revert; recover unloads; sound? asserts the invariant φ(γ) = γ₀ at any moment.

cordis.rb · lines 82–135

82  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
83  # Definition 2 — the effect context ∂Γ := Γ × (Γ → Γ).
84  #
85  # A pair (γ, φ): the current state and the *accumulator*, the composite of
86  # the inverses of the effects performed so far. The initial effect context
87  # is (γ₀, id_Γ). Soundness invariant (§3.1.1, after Theorem 7): φ(γ) = γ₀.
88  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
89  class EffectContext
90    # state is γ, the current context; accumulator is φ, which runs every
91    # tracked inverse and thereby recovers γ₀.
92    attr_reader :state, :accumulator
93
94    def initialize(state, accumulator = IDENTITY)
95      @state = state
96      @accumulator = accumulator
97      freeze
98    end
99
100    # Definition 3 — track_Γ(f, g)(γ, φ) = (f(γ), φ ∘ g): transform the state
101    # by f and compose the candidate inverse g onto the accumulator.
102    # Theorem 4: tracking is transparent to the forward state.
103    # Theorem 5: track is a monoid homomorphism from 𝔗_Γ into ∂Γ → ∂Γ.
104    def track(forward, inverse)
105      phi = @accumulator
106      EffectContext.new(forward.call(@state), ->(x) { phi.call(inverse.call(x)) })
107    end
108
109    # Definition 12 — effect_Γ(e)(γ, φ) = ((δ, φ ∘ g), track_Γ(g, pr₁ ∘ e)).
110    #
111    # Applies a Definition-8 effect and returns [successor, lifted_inverse].
112    # The lifted inverse is a transformation of ∂Γ in its own right: its
113    # forward map is g, and the way to undo *that* is to perform the effect
114    # again (pr₁ ∘ e) — undoing an effect is itself a trackable effect, which
115    # is what makes selective revert possible (Theorems 13–15).
116    def apply(effect)
117      delta, g = effect.call(@state)
118      phi = @accumulator
119      successor = EffectContext.new(delta, ->(x) { phi.call(g.call(x)) })
120      lifted_inverse = ->(ec) { ec.track(g, effect.forward) }
121      [successor, lifted_inverse]
122    end
123
124    # Definition 6 — recover_Γ(γ, φ) = (φ(γ), id_Γ): apply the accumulated
125    # inverses and reset. Theorem 7: tracked effects preserve the recovery
126    # result, so recover lands on the initial effect context (eq. 11).
127    def recover
128      EffectContext.new(@accumulator.call(@state), IDENTITY)
129    end
130
131    # The soundness invariant φ(γ) = γ₀ of a state in ∂Γ (§3.1.1).
132    def sound?(initial_state)
133      @accumulator.call(@state) == initial_state
134    end
135  end

Cordis::Independence

Definition 19, made checkable: commutation on generators, inverse-stability, both extensional over caller-supplied sample states. Key-disjoint effects on a table pass; same-key effects fail.

cordis.rb · lines 137–208

137  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
138  # Definitions 17 & 19 — transformation monoids and independence of effects.
139  #
140  # Two effects are independent when every transformation of one commutes with
141  # every transformation of the other (clause 1) and neither's transformations
142  # disturb the inverse the other yields (clause 2). Independence is what lets
143  # inverses run out of LIFO order: Corollary 21 recovers γ₀ under *any*
144  # permutation of the reverts — the formal licence for withdrawing one
145  # component from a running system (§3.1.3).
146  #
147  # Function equality is undecidable in general, so the check is extensional:
148  # Lemma 18 settles commutation on the generators, and we compare functions
149  # pointwise over a caller-supplied sample of states. Contexts are partial
150  # (Σ is a partial function): a violated precondition "produces no
151  # transition", so commutation compares undefined points as agreeing when
152  # both sides are undefined, and inverse-stability constrains only the
153  # points where an effect was actually applied.
154  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
155  module Independence
156    module_function
157
158    # The generators of 𝔐(e), sampled at the given states: the forward map
159    # pr₁ ∘ e together with every inverse e yields there (Definition 17). An
160    # effect undefined at a sample state yields no inverse there.
161    def generators(effect, states)
162      sampled = states.map { |gamma| attempt { effect.call(gamma)[1] } }
163      [effect.forward, *sampled.reject { |inverse| inverse == :undefined }]
164    end
165
166    def independent?(first, second, states:)
167      commutes?(first, second, states) &&
168        inverse_stable?(first, second, states) &&
169        inverse_stable?(second, first, states)
170    end
171
172    # Clause 1 of Definition 19, reduced to generators by Lemma 18.1.
173    def commutes?(first, second, states)
174      generators(first, states).all? do |f|
175        generators(second, states).all? do |g|
176          states.all? { |gamma| agree?(-> { f.call(g.call(gamma)) }, -> { g.call(f.call(gamma)) }) }
177        end
178      end
179    end
180
181    # Clause 2 of Definition 19: pr₂(e(g(γ))) = pr₂(e(γ)) for every
182    # transformation g of the other effect — foreign moves leave the yielded
183    # inverse unchanged (compared pointwise over the samples). Where either
184    # application is undefined the clause constrains nothing: there is no
185    # applied effect whose inverse could have been disturbed, and domain
186    # disagreements are already clause 1's business.
187    def inverse_stable?(effect, other, states)
188      generators(other, states).all? do |g|
189        states.all? do |gamma|
190          moved = attempt { effect.call(g.call(gamma))[1] }
191          home  = attempt { effect.call(gamma)[1] }
192          next true if moved == :undefined || home == :undefined
193
194          states.all? { |x| agree?(-> { moved.call(x) }, -> { home.call(x) }) }
195        end
196      end
197    end
198
199    def attempt(&block)
200      block.call
201    rescue PreconditionViolation, KeyError
202      :undefined
203    end
204
205    def agree?(left, right)
206      attempt(&left) == attempt(&right)
207    end
208  end

Spatial machinery — the dependency table as context

The coeffect context and its operations. Note what is absent: no subscription list, no event emitter. set returns an Effect, so the temporal machinery tracks provisions like any other write — that one design decision is the paper's synergy.

Cordis::Satisfaction · Cordis::CoeffectContext

cordis.rb · lines 210–217

210  # Shared observational structure of coeffect contexts: the satisfaction
211  # predicate σ ⊨ d := ∀k ∈ d. k ∈ dom(σ) (eq. 24) — decidable since dom(σ)
212  # is finite, and checked at every effect boundary (§3.2.2).
213  module Satisfaction
214    def satisfies?(spec)
215      spec.all? { |key| key?(key) }
216    end
217  end

cordis.rb · lines 219–284

219  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
220  # Definition 22 — the coeffect context Σ := (k : K) ⇀ V_k.
221  #
222  # A finite partial function assigning to each dependency key a typed value:
223  # the inversion-of-control container, formalized (§3.2.1). Extension and
224  # restriction carry preconditions — a dependency cannot be provided twice
225  # nor revoked if absent.
226  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
227  class CoeffectContext
228    include Satisfaction
229
230    attr_reader :table
231
232    def initialize(table = {})
233      @table = table.freeze
234      freeze
235    end
236
237    # σ(k) — application, defined when k ∈ dom(σ) (Definition 22).
238    def fetch(key)
239      raise PreconditionViolation, "get(#{key.inspect}): k ∉ dom(σ)" unless key?(key)
240
241      @table[key]
242    end
243
244    # k ∈ dom(σ) — membership (Definition 22).
245    def key?(key)
246      @table.key?(key)
247    end
248
249    # dom(σ).
250    def domain
251      @table.keys
252    end
253
254    # σ[k ↦ v] under the provision precondition k ∉ dom(σ) (Definition 23).
255    def bind(key, value)
256      raise PreconditionViolation, "set(#{key.inspect}): already provided (k ∈ dom(σ))" if key?(key)
257
258      write(key, value)
259    end
260
261    # σ \ k — restriction, defined when k ∈ dom(σ) (Definition 22).
262    def unbind(key)
263      raise PreconditionViolation, "revoke(#{key.inspect}): k ∉ dom(σ)" unless key?(key)
264
265      remaining = @table.dup
266      remaining.delete(key)
267      self.class.new(remaining)
268    end
269
270    # Raw table update σ[k ↦ v], agreeing with σ elsewhere (Definition 22) —
271    # no precondition; operation lifts rebind through it (eq. 23).
272    def write(key, value)
273      self.class.new(@table.merge(key => value))
274    end
275
276    def ==(other)
277      other.is_a?(self.class) && table == other.table
278    end
279    alias eql? ==
280
281    def hash
282      [self.class, table].hash
283    end
284  end

Cordis::Coeffects

cordis.rb · lines 286–321

286  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
287  # Definition 23 — the get and set operations on Σ, and Definition 26's
288  # classification of context transitions.
289  #
290  # set(k, v)(σ) = (σ[k ↦ v], λσ′. σ′ \ k) has type 𝔈*_Σ — precisely a
291  # witnessed effect function on the coeffect context. "This is the synergy
292  # between reactive coeffects and revertible effects: coeffect operations
293  # are effects, and effects are revertible" (§3.2.1).
294  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
295  module Coeffects
296    module_function
297
298    # get(k)(σ) = σ(k), requiring k ∈ dom(σ) (Definition 23).
299    def get(key)
300      ->(sigma) { sigma.fetch(key) }
301    end
302
303    # set(k, v) as a witnessed effect on Σ (Definition 23).
304    def set(key, value)
305      Effect.new("set(#{key.inspect})") do |sigma|
306        [sigma.bind(key, value), ->(next_sigma) { next_sigma.unbind(key) }]
307      end
308    end
309
310    # notify_d(σ, σ′) — Definition 26: classify a transition against a
311    # coeffect specification d (Definition 25, a set of keys) as activating,
312    # deactivating, or neutral.
313    def notify(spec, before, after)
314      was = before.satisfies?(spec)
315      now = after.satisfies?(spec)
316      return :activating if !was && now
317      return :deactivating if was && !now
318
319      :neutral
320    end
321  end

Cordis::Coeffect

The per-key triple (V_k, ≈_k, A_k): operations act on the value alone and lift to the whole context, inverses confined to their key.

cordis.rb · lines 323–363

323  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
324  # Definition 24 — a coeffect at a key k is a triple (V_k, ≈_k, A_k): the
325  # value type, an equivalence up to which values at k are compared, and the
326  # operations the value provides to a component holding it.
327  #
328  # An operation a ∈ A_k acts on the value alone (eq. 22) and is lifted to Σ
329  # by eq. 23: it reads the binding at k, transforms it, and its inverse
330  # applies the value-level inverse g to *whatever value σ′ then holds at k* —
331  # which is what confines the operation to its key and keeps it independent
332  # of every other key (§3.2.1, remark after eq. 23).
333  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
334  class Coeffect
335    attr_reader :key, :value_type, :equivalence
336
337    def initialize(key, value_type: Object, equivalence: ->(a, b) { a == b })
338      @key = key
339      @value_type = value_type
340      @equivalence = equivalence
341      @operations = {}
342    end
343
344    # Declare an operation a : X_a → V_k → V_k × (V_k ⇀ V_k) × B_a (eq. 22).
345    # The block maps (argument, value) to [new_value, value_inverse, outcome].
346    def operation(name, &body)
347      @operations[name] = body
348      self
349    end
350
351    # The lift a^Σ (eq. 23): defined when k ∈ dom(σ); its first two
352    # constituents form an effect function on Σ, and the third is the outcome
353    # b : B_a, paired with each application (eq. 22) — every call yields its
354    # own [σ′, inverse, outcome] triple.
355    def lift(name, argument)
356      body = @operations.fetch(name)
357      key = @key
358      Effect.new("#{key.inspect}.#{name}") do |sigma|
359        value, inverse, outcome = body.call(argument, sigma.fetch(key))
360        [sigma.write(key, value), ->(next_sigma) { next_sigma.write(key, inverse.call(next_sigma.fetch(key))) }, outcome]
361      end
362    end
363  end

Cordis::InterceptedContext

The two-layer (ι, σ) context: providers are functions of metadata, the component's declaration merges with what the context carries — right-biased, so an enclosing context can constrain a component without touching it.

cordis.rb · lines 439–510

439  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
440  # Definitions 30 & 31 — the coeffect context with interception:
441  # Σ^inter := ((k : K) → M_k) × ((k : K) ⇀ (M_k → V_k)), a pair (ι, σ) of
442  # context-carried metadata and provider functions, each key equipped with a
443  # metadata monoid (M_k, ⊕_k, ε_k).
444  #
445  # get(k, μ)(ι, σ) = σ(k)(μ ⊕_k ι(k)): the component-declared metadata μ is
446  # merged with the context-carried ι(k) and the provider function is applied
447  # to the result. The merge is right-biased, so ι(k) takes priority — an
448  # enclosing context can constrain how a component uses a coeffect without
449  # modifying that component (§6.3). intercept(k, ν) derives a fresh context
450  # merging ν onto the inherited metadata, the provider table untouched —
451  # like isolate, a derived realization (Definition 27) with nothing to track.
452  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
453  class InterceptedContext
454    include Satisfaction
455
456    EMPTY = {}.freeze
457    MERGE = ->(left, right) { left.merge(right) } # ⊕_k for hash metadata; right-biased
458
459    attr_reader :metadata, :providers # ι, σ
460
461    def initialize(metadata = {}, providers = {}, merge: MERGE, empty: EMPTY)
462      @metadata = metadata.freeze
463      @providers = providers.freeze
464      @merge = merge
465      @empty = empty
466      freeze
467    end
468
469    # get(k, μ)(ι, σ) = σ(k)(μ ⊕_k ι(k)) — Definition 31, eq. 30.
470    def fetch(key, declared = @empty)
471      raise PreconditionViolation, "get(#{key.inspect}): k ∉ dom(σ)" unless key?(key)
472
473      @providers[key].call(@merge.call(declared, @metadata.fetch(key, @empty)))
474    end
475
476    def key?(key)
477      @providers.key?(key)
478    end
479
480    # set(k, ψ) — an effect on the provider table under the Definition 23
481    # precondition, its inverse the restriction λσ′. σ′ \ k (eq. 30).
482    def bind(key, provider)
483      raise PreconditionViolation, "set(#{key.inspect}): already provided" if key?(key)
484
485      self.class.new(@metadata, @providers.merge(key => provider), merge: @merge, empty: @empty)
486    end
487
488    def unbind(key)
489      raise PreconditionViolation, "revoke(#{key.inspect}): k ∉ dom(σ)" unless key?(key)
490
491      remaining = @providers.dup
492      remaining.delete(key)
493      self.class.new(@metadata, remaining, merge: @merge, empty: @empty)
494    end
495
496    # intercept(k, ν)(ι, σ) = (ι[k ↦ ι(k) ⊕_k ν], σ) — derived, no inverse.
497    def intercept(key, extra)
498      merged = @merge.call(@metadata.fetch(key, @empty), extra)
499      self.class.new(@metadata.merge(key => merged), @providers, merge: @merge, empty: @empty)
500    end
501
502    def ==(other)
503      other.is_a?(self.class) && metadata == other.metadata && providers == other.providers
504    end
505    alias eql? ==
506
507    def hash
508      [self.class, metadata, providers].hash
509    end
510  end

Cordis::IsolatedContext

The two-layer context (ρ, σ): keys resolve through realms before touching the table. isolate derives instead of mutating — nothing to track, recovery is discard.

cordis.rb · lines 365–437

365  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
366  # Definition 28 — the coeffect context with isolation Σ^iso := (ρ, σ).
367  #
368  # Two layers: ρ assigns realm identifiers to keys (a key outside dom(ρ)
369  # resolves to its own realm), σ maps realm identifiers to values. The same
370  # logical key can bind different values in different contexts — runtime
371  # ad-hoc polymorphism for sandboxes, tenants, and tests (§3.2.3).
372  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
373  class IsolatedContext
374    include Satisfaction
375
376    attr_reader :realms, :table # ρ, σ
377
378    def initialize(realms = {}, table = {})
379      @realms = realms.freeze
380      @table = table.freeze
381      freeze
382    end
383
384    # ρ(k), with ρ(k) = k for keys outside dom(ρ) (Definition 28).
385    def resolve(key)
386      @realms.fetch(key, key)
387    end
388
389    # get(k)(ρ, σ) = σ(ρ(k)) (Definition 29), precondition ρ(k) ∈ dom(σ).
390    def fetch(key)
391      raise PreconditionViolation, "get(#{key.inspect}): ρ(k) ∉ dom(σ)" unless key?(key)
392
393      @table[resolve(key)]
394    end
395
396    def key?(key)
397      @table.key?(resolve(key))
398    end
399
400    # set(k, v) transported along ρ (Definition 29): writes σ[ρ(k) ↦ v] under
401    # the precondition ρ(k) ∉ dom(σ). Per eq. 28 the inverse restricts at
402    # ρ′(k) — the realm is re-resolved in the context the inverse is applied
403    # to, which is exactly what unbind below does on its receiver.
404    def bind(key, value)
405      realm = resolve(key)
406      raise PreconditionViolation, "set(#{key.inspect}): already provided in realm #{realm.inspect}" if @table.key?(realm)
407
408      self.class.new(@realms, @table.merge(realm => value))
409    end
410
411    def unbind(key)
412      realm = resolve(key)
413      raise PreconditionViolation, "revoke(#{key.inspect}): ρ(k) ∉ dom(σ)" unless @table.key?(realm)
414
415      remaining = @table.dup
416      remaining.delete(realm)
417      self.class.new(@realms, remaining)
418    end
419
420    # isolate(k, r)(ρ, σ) = (ρ[k ↦ r], σ) — Definition 29. A *derived*
421    # realization (Definition 27): it leaves the shared table as it stands and
422    # returns a fresh context deriving from it, with the identity as inverse —
423    # there is no inverse to track, and recovery discards the derived context.
424    # A key already isolated is reassigned rather than refused.
425    def isolate(key, realm)
426      self.class.new(@realms.merge(key => realm), @table)
427    end
428
429    def ==(other)
430      other.is_a?(self.class) && realms == other.realms && table == other.table
431    end
432    alias eql? ==
433
434    def hash
435      [self.class, realms, table].hash
436    end
437  end

The reactive runtime — where both dimensions meet

Component declares its specification; System makes the reactive invariant operational: every mutation is a classified transition, activation runs effects with tracking, deactivation replays the component's own accumulator, and withdrawals are ordered after the deactivations they cause.

Cordis::Component · Cordis::ActivationScope

cordis.rb · lines 512–532

512  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
513  # A component — the unit of dynamic composition (§1.3, contribution 4).
514  #
515  # It declares the coeffects it requires as a specification (Definition 25)
516  # and expresses what it does to the context as witnessed effects performed
517  # on activation. Its lifecycle is not called; it *reacts* (Definition 26).
518  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
519  class Component
520    attr_reader :name, :requires
521
522    def initialize(name, requires: [], &activation)
523      @name = name
524      @requires = requires.freeze
525      @activation = activation
526      freeze
527    end
528
529    def activate(scope)
530      @activation&.call(scope)
531    end
532  end

cordis.rb · lines 534–556

534  # What an activating component sees: reads through get (Definition 23) and
535  # provisions through witnessed set effects, each inverse joining the
536  # component's own accumulator (§3.1.3: "one sequence may interleave the
537  # effects of several components, each keeping the inverses of its own").
538  class ActivationScope
539    def initialize(system, entry)
540      @system = system
541      @entry = entry
542    end
543
544    def get(key)
545      Coeffects.get(key).call(@system.context)
546    end
547
548    def provide(key, value)
549      @system.perform(Coeffects.set(key, value), @entry, undo_tag: key)
550    end
551
552    # Perform any witnessed effect (e.g. a Coeffect operation lift, eq. 23).
553    def apply(effect)
554      @system.perform(effect, @entry)
555    end

Cordis::System

cordis.rb · lines 558–724

558  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
559  # The reactive runtime — the reactive invariant of §3.2.2 made operational.
560  #
561  # Every mutation of the shared context is an effect function; every
562  # transition it produces is classified against every mounted component's
563  # specification (Definition 26). An activating transition runs the
564  # component's effects with full tracking; a deactivating transition runs
565  # its accumulator, in LIFO order (Theorem 16). A withdrawal is ordered
566  # *after* the deactivations it causes (§3.2.2, closing paragraph — the
567  # excerpt defers the formal machinery to §4.3.1); reverting one component's
568  # accumulator out of global order is licensed by independence: components
569  # touch disjoint keys, so Corollary 21 applies.
570  # ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
571  class System
572    Entry = Struct.new(:component, :undo, :active, :host)
573
574    attr_reader :context, :initial, :trace
575
576    def initialize(context = CoeffectContext.new)
577      @context = context
578      @initial = context
579      @entries = {}
580      @trace = []
581    end
582
583    def active
584      @entries.values.select { |entry| entry.active && !entry.host }.map { |entry| entry.component.name }
585    end
586
587    # Component arrival. Mounting does not transform σ; the component
588    # activates immediately iff σ already satisfies its specification —
589    # "a component activates only at a state satisfying its specification,
590    # so it never reads a binding that is absent" (§3.2.2). Mounting an
591    # already-mounted name would orphan its accumulator, so it is refused —
592    # the same discipline as the no-double-provision precondition.
593    def mount(component)
594      raise PreconditionViolation, "mount(#{component.name}): already mounted" if @entries.key?(component.name)
595
596      entry = Entry.new(component, [], false)
597      @entries[component.name] = entry
598      log "mount    #{component.name}  (requires #{component.requires.inspect})"
599      activate(entry) if @context.satisfies?(component.requires)
600      self
601    end
602
603    # Component departure: unloading is applying the component's accumulator
604    # (§3.1.3, closing criterion), then forgetting it.
605    def unmount(name)
606      entry = @entries.fetch(name)
607      log "unmount  #{name}"
608      deactivate(entry) if entry.active
609      @entries.delete(name)
610      self
611    end
612
613    # A provision made by the host environment rather than a component.
614    def provide(key, value)
615      perform(Coeffects.set(key, value), host_entry, undo_tag: key)
616    end
617
618    # Host-side withdrawal of a provision — realized as the *selective revert*
619    # of the recorded set effect: its witnessed inverse is applied at a state
620    # later effects have moved, exactly the situation Corollary 21 covers.
621    def revoke(key)
622      entry = host_entry
623      index = entry.undo.rindex { |(undo_key, _)| undo_key == key }
624      raise PreconditionViolation, "revoke(#{key.inspect}): the host provided no such key" unless index
625
626      _, inverse = entry.undo.delete_at(index)
627      transition("revert set(#{key.inspect})", inverse)
628      self
629    end
630
631    # Witnessed application (Definition 8): σ ↦ (δ, g). The successor becomes
632    # the shared context; the inverse joins the owner's accumulator (§3.1.3),
633    # tagged so a selective revert can find it again.
634    def perform(effect, entry, undo_tag: nil)
635      inverse = transition(effect.name, effect)
636      entry.undo << [undo_tag, inverse] if inverse
637      self
638    end
639
640    private
641
642    def host_entry
643      @entries[:host] ||= Entry.new(Component.new("host"), [], true, true)
644    end
645
646    # One reactive step. `step` is an Effect or a bare inverse Γ → Γ.
647    def transition(label, step)
648      probe = probe_successor(step)
649      # §3.2.2: order the withdrawal after the deactivations it causes.
650      (@context.domain - probe.domain).each { |key| deactivate_dependents(key) }
651      before = @context
652      after, inverse = run_step(step, before)
653      @context = after
654      log "effect   #{label}"
655      begin
656        classify(before, after)
657      rescue StandardError
658        # A failed cascade must not strand the effect that triggered it.
659        # Withdrawing it is itself a classified transition, so successful
660        # activations unwind in dependency order and "a violated precondition
661        # produces no transition" (§3.2.1) holds one level up as well.
662        transition("rollback #{label}", inverse) if inverse
663        raise
664      end
665      inverse
666    end
667
668    def probe_successor(step)
669      step.is_a?(Effect) ? step.call(@context).first : step.call(@context)
670    end
671
672    def run_step(step, sigma)
673      step.is_a?(Effect) ? step.call(sigma) : [step.call(sigma), nil]
674    end
675
676    # Definition 26 applied to every mounted specification. Deactivations were
677    # ordered before the transition; what remains to react to is activation.
678    def classify(before, after)
679      @entries.each_value do |entry|
680        next if entry.host
681
682        verdict = Coeffects.notify(entry.component.requires, before, after)
683        activate(entry) if verdict == :activating && !entry.active
684        deactivate(entry) if verdict == :deactivating && entry.active # defensive; pre-ordered above
685      end
686    end
687
688    # The reactive invariant, activating half: "an activating transition
689    # triggers execution of the component's effects (with full effect
690    # tracking)" (§3.2.2, after Definition 26). A component whose activation
691    # fails is deactivated on the spot — its partial effects are recovered
692    # from its own accumulator — and the failure propagates to the transition
693    # that triggered it.
694    def activate(entry)
695      entry.active = true
696      log "activate #{entry.component.name}"
697      entry.component.activate(ActivationScope.new(self, entry))
698    rescue StandardError
699      deactivate(entry)
700      raise
701    end
702
703    # Deactivating half: "a deactivating transition triggers recovery by
704    # applying the accumulator" — the component's own inverses, in LIFO order
705    # (Theorem 16), each application itself a classified transition.
706    def deactivate(entry)
707      entry.active = false
708      log "deactivate #{entry.component.name}"
709      while (tag_and_inverse = entry.undo.pop)
710        tag, inverse = tag_and_inverse
711        transition(tag ? "revert set(#{tag.inspect})" : "revert #{entry.component.name} effect", inverse)
712      end
713    end
714
715    def deactivate_dependents(key)
716      @entries.each_value do |entry|
717        deactivate(entry) if entry.active && !entry.host && entry.component.requires.include?(key)
718      end
719    end
720
721    def log(line)
722      @trace << line
723    end
724  end

The fiber calculus — §4 as a state machine you can run

cordis_calculus.rb implements the operational semantics directly: fibers with the four-state lifecycle, the derived context σγ, target and committed views, and the ten rules as methods that fire when their premises hold. The driver picks applicable rules at random — the calculus names no scheduler, so the seed is the scheduling policy — and the metatheory is then a set of assertions over sampled schedules.

Component · Fiber

cordis_calculus.rb · lines 1–31

1# frozen_string_literal: true
2
3# The calculus of dynamic composition (§4), executable.
4#
5# Section 4 decomposes a running system into fibers — instantiations of
6# components carrying lifecycle state — and gives ten rules that move them.
7# This file implements that operational semantics directly: the state γ is a
8# registry of fibers (Definition 45), the coeffect context is derived from
9# the Active fibers (eq. 40), the target view drives every transition
10# (Definition 46), and the rules of §4.2–§4.3 are methods that fire when
11# their premises hold. calculus_checks.rb runs the metatheory of §4.4
12# against random schedules.
13#
14# Modeling choices, stated once:
15# - Effects are iterators (Definition 51): a component's body is a sequence
16#   of steps, one consumed per L-Iter, each yielding its inverse; a step is
17#   a provision into the fiber's own table (confinement, Definition 48), a
18#   registration of a child component (Definition 47), or a raise (§4.3.4).
19# - Steps are atomic, so the aborting and landing alternatives of L-Divert
20#   coincide at every iteration boundary (§4.3.3's inertia is the statement
21#   that only the landing alternative exists once an iteration is in
22#   flight; with atomic iterations the boundary is always at hand).
23# - Names are atoms the rules only compare (§4.1); we draw registration
24#   names deterministically per (parent, component, occurrence) so that two
25#   schedules of the same orchestration steps are comparable without the
26#   renaming of Lemma 56.
27
28require_relative "cordis"
29
30module Cordis
31  module Calculus

cordis_calculus.rb · lines 32–73

32    # Definition 43 — a component over Γ is a triple (d, p, e): the coeffect
33    # specification, the provision, and the effect function, here an iterator
34    # given as a list of steps.
35    class Component
36      attr_reader :id, :inject, :provide, :steps
37
38      def initialize(id, inject: [], provide: [], steps: [])
39        @id = id
40        @inject = inject.freeze
41        @provide = provide.freeze
42        @steps = steps.freeze
43        freeze
44      end
45
46      # Small builder: set/register/fail! append steps (Definition 51's
47      # iterations, in order).
48      def self.build(id, inject: [], provide: [])
49        builder = StepBuilder.new
50        yield builder if block_given?
51        new(id, inject: inject, provide: provide, steps: builder.steps)
52      end
53
54      class StepBuilder
55        attr_reader :steps
56
57        def initialize
58          @steps = []
59        end
60
61        def set(key, value = nil, &block)
62          @steps << [:set, key, block || value]
63        end
64
65        def register(component, config = nil)
66          @steps << [:register, component, config]
67        end
68
69        def fail!(error)
70          @steps << [:fail, error]
71        end
72      end
73    end

cordis_calculus.rb · lines 75–108

75    # Definition 44 — a fiber ⟨d, p, e, π, σ, τ, θ⟩: the component fields, the
76    # parent, the fiber's own coeffect table, the retirement flag, and the
77    # lifecycle state θ (Definition 49):
78    #   Inactive(ζ) | Reloading(i, g, ω) | Active(g, ω) | Unloading(g, ω, ζ)
79    # where i is the remaining iterator, g the accumulator built so far
80    # (a stack of inverses, applied LIFO), ω the committed view d → 𝔑, and ζ
81    # the outcome — ⊥ or an error (§4.3.4).
82    class Fiber
83      attr_reader :component, :parent
84      attr_accessor :table, :retired, :state, :remaining, :accumulator, :committed, :outcome
85
86      def initialize(component, parent)
87        @component = component
88        @parent = parent
89        @table = {}        # σ_n — empty until its effects run (Definition 44)
90        @retired = false   # τ_n
91        @state = :inactive # θ_n
92        @remaining = nil   # i — remaining iterations while Reloading
93        @accumulator = []  # g — inverses, most recent last
94        @committed = nil   # ω — the committed view
95        @outcome = nil     # ζ — ⊥ (nil) or an error
96      end
97
98      def inject = component.inject
99      def provide = component.provide
100
101      # installed_n(γ) := θ_n ≠ Inactive(−)  (eq. 44)
102      def installed? = state != :inactive
103
104      def failed? = state == :inactive && !outcome.nil?
105
106      # Lemma 57 — a vestigial entry: retired, Inactive(⊥), empty table.
107      def vestigial? = retired && state == :inactive && outcome.nil? && table.empty?
108    end

Machine — the registry and the ten rules

cordis_calculus.rb · lines 110–425

110    # The state γ: the registry F_γ (Definition 45) plus the rule engine.
111    class Machine
112      attr_reader :fibers, :trace
113
114      def initialize(seed: 0)
115        @fibers = {}
116        @trace = []
117        @rng = Random.new(seed)
118        @registration_counters = Hash.new(0)
119      end
120
121      # --- Definition 45 / eq. 40: the derived coeffect context -------------
122      # σ_γ := ⋃ { σ_m | θ_m = Active(−, −) } — Active fibers alone, which is
123      # what makes a withdrawal visible one step before it happens (L-Leave).
124      def sigma
125        @fibers.values.select { |f| f.state == :active }.each_with_object({}) do |fiber, table|
126          table.merge!(fiber.table)
127        end
128      end
129
130      # provider_k(γ) — unique because provisions are disjoint (Definition 43).
131      def provider(key)
132        @fibers.find { |_, f| f.state == :active && f.table.key?(key) }&.first
133      end
134
135      # --- Definition 46 / eq. 41: the target view --------------------------
136      # ⊥ when the fiber ought not to be running; otherwise each declared key
137      # mapped to its provider.
138      def target(name)
139        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
140        return nil if fiber.retired
141
142        view = {}
143        fiber.inject.each do |key|
144          provider_name = provider(key)
145          return nil unless provider_name
146
147          view[key] = provider_name
148        end
149        view
150      end
151
152      # --- eq. 45: quiescence on the four-state space ------------------------
153      def quiet?
154        @fibers.all? do |name, fiber|
155          case fiber.state
156          when :inactive then !fiber.outcome.nil? || target(name).nil?
157          when :active then target(name) == fiber.committed
158          else false
159          end
160        end
161      end
162
163      # --- Definition 50 / eq. 46: relied upon --------------------------------
164      # Some other installed fiber resolves a key to n through its committed view.
165      def relied?(name)
166        @fibers.any? do |m, fiber|
167          m != name && fiber.installed? && fiber.committed&.value?(name)
168        end
169      end
170
171      # --- Definition 58: well-formedness (checked by Theorem 59's client) ---
172      def well_formed?
173        @fibers.all? do |name, fiber|
174          parent_ok = fiber.parent == :root || @fibers.key?(fiber.parent)
175          disjoint = @fibers.none? { |m, other| m != name && other.provide.intersect?(fiber.provide) }
176          parent_ok && disjoint && committed_view_well_formed?(fiber)
177        end
178      end
179
180      # Clauses (3) and (4) of Definition 58: an installed fiber's committed
181      # view is total on its specification, valued in the registry, and names
182      # installed providers only.
183      def committed_view_well_formed?(fiber)
184        return true unless fiber.installed?
185
186        fiber.inject.all? { |k| @fibers.key?(fiber.committed&.[](k)) } &&
187          fiber.committed.values.all? { |m| @fibers[m].installed? }
188      end
189
190      # ====================== orchestration rules (γ ⇒ δ) =====================
191
192      # O-Insert: freshness, parent present, provisions disjoint from every
193      # fiber's (the single-source discipline).
194      def o_insert(name, component, parent: :root)
195        premise !@fibers.key?(name), "O-Insert: #{name} not fresh"
196        premise parent == :root || @fibers.key?(parent), "O-Insert: parent absent"
197        clash = @fibers.values.any? { |f| f.provide.intersect?(component.provide) }
198        premise !clash, "O-Insert: provisions not disjoint"
199        @fibers[name] = Fiber.new(component, parent)
200        log :o_insert, name
201        self
202      end
203
204      # O-Retire: unconditional on the fiber's state — a request; the
205      # lifecycle rules carry it out.
206      def o_retire(name)
207        premise @fibers.key?(name), "O-Retire: #{name} absent"
208        @fibers[name].retired = true
209        log :o_retire, name
210        self
211      end
212
213      # O-Remove: retired, Inactive, and childless — removing earlier would
214      # discard the accumulator and leak.
215      def o_remove(name)
216        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
217        premise fiber.retired, "O-Remove: not retired"
218        premise fiber.state == :inactive, "O-Remove: not Inactive"
219        premise @fibers.none? { |_, f| f.parent == name }, "O-Remove: has children"
220        @fibers.delete(name)
221        log :o_remove, name
222        self
223      end
224
225      # ======================= lifecycle rules (γ ⟶ δ) ========================
226      # Each returns true if its premises held and it fired.
227      # rubocop:disable Naming/PredicateMethod -- the names mirror the paper's rules
228
229      # L-Begin: Inactive(⊥) with a target view — commit the view, start the
230      # iterator with an empty accumulator.
231      def l_begin(name)
232        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
233        view = target(name)
234        return false unless fiber.state == :inactive && fiber.outcome.nil? && view
235
236        fiber.state = :reloading
237        fiber.remaining = fiber.component.steps.dup
238        fiber.accumulator = []
239        fiber.committed = view
240        log :l_begin, name
241        true
242      end
243
244      # L-Iter / L-Finish: run one iteration while the committed view is still
245      # the target view; the yielded inverse composes onto the accumulator
246      # (LIFO); the final iteration lands in Active.
247      def l_step(name)
248        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
249        return false unless fiber.state == :reloading && target(name) == fiber.committed
250        return false if next_step_raises?(fiber)
251
252        if fiber.remaining.empty?
253          fiber.state = :active
254          fiber.remaining = nil
255          log :l_finish, name
256        else
257          run_iteration(fiber, fiber.remaining.shift)
258          log :l_iter, name
259        end
260        true
261      end
262
263      # L-Divert: the target view turned during the transition — route through
264      # Unloading with the accumulator built so far (the aborting alternative;
265      # see the modeling note at the top).
266      def l_divert(name)
267        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
268        return false unless fiber.state == :reloading && target(name) != fiber.committed
269
270        fiber.state = :unloading
271        fiber.remaining = nil
272        fiber.outcome = nil
273        log :l_divert, name
274        true
275      end
276
277      # L-Raise: an iteration raises in place of yielding (§4.3.4, eq. 49);
278      # not conditioned on the target view at all.
279      def l_raise(name)
280        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
281        return false unless fiber.state == :reloading && next_step_raises?(fiber)
282
283        error = fiber.remaining.first[1]
284        fiber.state = :unloading
285        fiber.remaining = nil
286        fiber.outcome = error
287        log :l_raise, name
288        true
289      end
290
291      # L-Leave: record the decision to deactivate without acting on it — the
292      # fiber stops providing (σ_γ unions Active alone) while its committed
293      # view, and everyone else's, stays intact.
294      def l_leave(name)
295        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
296        return false unless fiber.state == :active && target(name) != fiber.committed
297
298        fiber.state = :unloading
299        log :l_leave, name
300        true
301      end
302
303      # L-Unload: guarded by ¬relied_n(γ) — the withdrawal of a key takes
304      # effect only after every consumer that resolved it here has gone
305      # (Definition 50). The one rule that applies an accumulator.
306      def l_unload(name)
307        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
308        return false unless fiber.state == :unloading && !relied?(name)
309
310        fiber.accumulator.reverse_each(&:call) # LIFO recovery (Theorem 16)
311        fiber.accumulator = []
312        fiber.committed = nil
313        fiber.state = :inactive
314        log :l_unload, name
315        true
316      end
317
318      # rubocop:enable Naming/PredicateMethod
319
320      # ============================ the driver =================================
321
322      LIFECYCLE_RULES = %i[l_begin l_step l_divert l_raise l_leave l_unload].freeze
323
324      # One nondeterministic lifecycle step — an action that reports whether
325      # any rule fired, so `step` keeps the paper's word for it. Collect every
326      # (rule, fiber) whose premises hold and fire one at random: "no rule
327      # mentions a scheduler; a theorem proved over all sequences holds for
328      # every scheduling policy" (§4.2) — the seed picks the policy.
329      # rubocop:disable Naming/PredicateMethod
330      def step
331        applicable = @fibers.keys.flat_map do |name|
332          LIFECYCLE_RULES.select { |rule| applicable?(rule, name) }.map { |rule| [rule, name] }
333        end
334        return false if applicable.empty?
335
336        rule, name = applicable[@rng.rand(applicable.length)]
337        send(rule, name)
338        true
339      end
340      # rubocop:enable Naming/PredicateMethod
341
342      # Run lifecycle steps to quiescence. Theorem 66 bounds every maximal
343      # sequence, so the cap is a defect detector, not a terminator.
344      def run(max_steps: 10_000)
345        steps = 0
346        while step
347          steps += 1
348          raise "progress violated: #{steps} steps without quiescence" if steps > max_steps
349        end
350        raise "stuck: no rule applies but the state is not quiescent" unless quiet?
351
352        self
353      end
354
355      # Snapshot for ≈-comparison (eq. 53 read at quiescence): every
356      # non-vestigial fiber's identity and state; Lemma 57 is why vestigial
357      # entries may be dropped.
358      def snapshot
359        @fibers.reject { |_, f| f.vestigial? }.to_h do |name, fiber|
360          [name, [fiber.component.id, fiber.parent, fiber.retired, fiber.table,
361                  fiber.state, fiber.committed, fiber.outcome]]
362        end
363      end
364
365      private
366
367      # rubocop:disable Metrics/AbcSize, Metrics/CyclomaticComplexity, Metrics/PerceivedComplexity -- one case arm per rule
368      def applicable?(rule, name)
369        fiber = @fibers.fetch(name)
370        view = target(name)
371        case rule
372        when :l_begin then fiber.state == :inactive && fiber.outcome.nil? && !view.nil?
373        when :l_step then fiber.state == :reloading && view == fiber.committed && !next_step_raises?(fiber)
374        when :l_divert then fiber.state == :reloading && view != fiber.committed
375        when :l_raise then fiber.state == :reloading && next_step_raises?(fiber)
376        when :l_leave then fiber.state == :active && view != fiber.committed
377        when :l_unload then fiber.state == :unloading && !relied?(name)
378        end
379      end
380      # rubocop:enable Metrics/AbcSize, Metrics/CyclomaticComplexity, Metrics/PerceivedComplexity
381
382      def next_step_raises?(fiber)
383        fiber.remaining&.first&.first == :fail
384      end
385
386      # One iteration of the effect iterator: perform the step, push its
387      # inverse. Reads go through the committed view ω — the resolution the
388      # transition committed to (Definition 46), not the live store.
389      def run_iteration(fiber, step)
390        kind, a, b = step
391        case kind
392        when :set
393          value = b.respond_to?(:call) ? b.call(reads_of(fiber)) : b
394          fiber.table[a] = value
395          fiber.accumulator << -> { fiber.table.delete(a) }
396        when :register
397          child = register(fiber, a, b)
398          fiber.accumulator << -> { o_retire(child) } # the inverse retires, never removes (Definition 47)
399        end
400      end
401
402      def reads_of(fiber)
403        fiber.committed.to_h { |key, provider_name| [key, @fibers.fetch(provider_name).table[key]] }
404      end
405
406      # Definition 47 — registration: an O-Insert taken by an iteration, with
407      # π = n; the name is drawn fresh and deterministically (see header note).
408      def register(fiber, component, _config)
409        parent_name = @fibers.key(fiber)
410        counter_key = [parent_name, component.id]
411        occurrence = @registration_counters[counter_key]
412        @registration_counters[counter_key] += 1
413        child_name = "#{parent_name}/#{component.id}##{occurrence}"
414        o_insert(child_name, component, parent: parent_name)
415        child_name
416      end
417
418      def premise(condition, message)
419        raise PreconditionViolation, message unless condition
420      end
421
422      def log(rule, name)
423        @trace << [rule, name]
424      end
425    end

The loader — declarative configuration, reconciliation, HMR

cordis_loader.rb: entries are the authoritative record (Definition 74); reconcile diffs them by id and dispatches the least disruptive operation per changed field. The hot-module-replacement phases (Algorithms 8–9) are pure functions over the module-import graph.

Entry · Reconciler

cordis_loader.rb · lines 18–24

18    # Definition 74 — an entry declares a single fiber: a stable id (the
19    # reconciliation key), the component to instantiate (here a builder
20    # applied to config, standing in for the url's module), the config bound
21    # into the effect function, and the administrative disabled flag.
22    Entry = Struct.new(:id, :builder, :config, :disabled, keyword_init: true) do
23      def component = builder.call(config)
24    end

cordis_loader.rb · lines 26–89

26    # The loader keeps the configuration tree authoritative: reconcile diffs
27    # the new entries against what is loaded, dispatches per field, and runs
28    # the machine to quiescence.
29    class Reconciler
30      attr_reader :machine
31
32      def initialize(machine = Calculus::Machine.new)
33        @machine = machine
34        @loaded = {} # id => Entry
35      end
36
37      def reconcile(entries)
38        desired = entries.to_h { |e| [e.id, e] }
39        (@loaded.keys - desired.keys).each { |id| remove(id) }
40        desired.each { |id, entry| reconcile_entry(id, entry) }
41        @machine.run
42        self
43      end
44
45      private
46
47      # Per-field dispatch (§5.2.1): a changed config or component rebuilds
48      # the entry — the least disruptive operation that realizes the new
49      # record; disabled unloads the fiber when set and reloads it when
50      # cleared; an unchanged entry is left exactly as it stands.
51      def reconcile_entry(id, entry)
52        previous = @loaded[id]
53        if previous.nil?
54          insert(entry) unless entry.disabled
55        elsif material_change?(previous, entry)
56          remove(id)
57          insert(entry) unless entry.disabled
58        end
59        @loaded[id] = entry
60      end
61
62      def material_change?(previous, entry)
63        previous.config != entry.config || previous.builder != entry.builder ||
64          previous.disabled != entry.disabled
65      end
66
67      def insert(entry)
68        @machine.o_insert(entry.id, entry.component)
69      end
70
71      # A rebuild withdraws what the fiber installed and leaves the fibers
72      # around it as they were (Corollary 62): retire, drive to Inactive,
73      # remove — O-Remove's premises are what make the order safe.
74      def remove(id)
75        return unless @machine.fibers.key?(id)
76
77        @machine.o_retire(id)
78        @machine.run
79        @machine.fibers.keys.select { |n| n == id || n.start_with?("#{id}/") }.reverse_each do |name|
80          @machine.o_remove(name) if removable?(name)
81        end
82        @loaded.delete(id)
83      end
84
85      def removable?(name)
86        fiber = @machine.fibers[name]
87        fiber&.retired && fiber.state == :inactive && @machine.fibers.none? { |_, f| f.parent == name }
88      end
89    end

HMR — classification and stale-entry detection

cordis_loader.rb · lines 91–157

91    # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
92    # Hot module replacement (§5.2.2) — the pure phases.
93    # imports: url → the modules that url directly imports.
94    # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
95    module HMR
96      module_function
97
98      # Algorithm 8 — module classification. Seeded with the imports of the
99      # stashed files, the fixed point accepts a module once one of its
100      # imports is accepted and declines one once all of its imports are
101      # declined; anything left undecided (an import cycle) defaults to
102      # declined.
103      def classify(stashed, externals, imports)
104        accepted = stashed.dup
105        declined = externals.dup
106        pending = []
107        stashed.each { |url| pending |= imports.fetch(url, []) - (accepted | declined) }
108        loop do
109          progress = false
110          pending.dup.each do |url|
111            deps = imports.fetch(url, [])
112            if deps.intersect?(accepted)
113              accepted |= [url]
114              pending -= [url]
115              progress = true
116            elsif (deps - declined).empty?
117              declined |= [url]
118              pending -= [url]
119              progress = true
120            else
121              pending |= deps - (accepted | declined)
122            end
123          end
124          break unless progress
125        end
126        [accepted, declined | pending]
127      end
128
129      # Algorithm 9 — stale-entry detection: the transitive imports of an
130      # entry's module, respecting declined as a boundary; an entry is stale
131      # exactly when its tree intersects accepted, and that tree is then
132      # folded into accepted.
133      def dependencies(root, declined, imports)
134        deps = []
135        traverse = lambda do |url|
136          return if deps.include?(url) || declined.include?(url)
137
138          deps << url
139          imports.fetch(url, []).each { |child| traverse.call(child) }
140        end
141        traverse.call(root)
142        deps
143      end
144
145      def detect(entry_urls, accepted, declined, imports)
146        accepted = accepted.dup
147        stale = []
148        entry_urls.each do |url|
149          tree = dependencies(url, declined, imports)
150          next unless tree.intersect?(accepted)
151
152          accepted |= tree
153          stale << url
154        end
155        [stale, accepted]
156      end
157    end

The theorems, executed

Two suites. theorem_checks.rb verifies §3's results on concrete contexts — extensional equality over probe states, exactly as far as running code can witness a proof — plus regression checks on the runtime's failure discipline. calculus_checks.rb runs §4's metatheory and §5.2's loader guarantees over randomized schedules. Excerpts, then both runs.

The canonical witnessed effect

assign chooses its inverse per state — restore the previous value, or delete the key if there was none. This is the effect the checks throw at every theorem.

theorem_checks.rb · lines 39–73

39# --- Sample contexts and effects --------------------------------------------
40#
41# Γ is a frozen Hash. `assign` is the canonical witnessed effect: its inverse
42# is chosen per state (Definition 8) — it restores whatever the key held at
43# the moment of application, or deletes the key if it was absent.
44
45def hash_write(gamma, key, value)
46  gamma.merge(key => value).freeze
47end
48
49def hash_delete(gamma, key)
50  gamma.reject { |k, _| k == key }.freeze
51end
52
53def assign(key, value)
54  Cordis::Effect.new("assign(#{key}, #{value})") do |gamma|
55    inverse =
56      if gamma.key?(key)
57        previous = gamma[key]
58        ->(x) { hash_write(x, key, previous) }
59      else
60        ->(x) { hash_delete(x, key) }
61      end
62    [hash_write(gamma, key, value), inverse]
63  end
64end
65
66def increment(key)
67  Cordis::Effect.new("increment(#{key})") do |gamma|
68    [hash_write(gamma, key, gamma.fetch(key) + 1), ->(x) { hash_write(x, key, x.fetch(key) - 1) }]
69  end
70end
71
72GAMMA0 = { a: 1, b: 2, c: 10 }.freeze
73PROBES = [GAMMA0, { a: 5, b: 2, c: 0 }.freeze, { a: 1, b: 7, c: 3, d: 4 }.freeze].freeze

Any-order withdrawal, all six permutations

theorem_checks.rb · lines 208–225

208check "Corollary 21: pairwise independent effects revert to γ₀ under every permutation" do
209  effects = { a: E_ASSIGN_A, c: E_INC_C, d: E_ASSIGN_D }
210  effects.values.combination(2) { |e1, e2| assert Cordis::Independence.independent?(e1, e2, states: PROBES) }
211
212  ec0 = Cordis::EffectContext.new(GAMMA0)
213  final = nil
214  undos = {}
215  effects.each do |key, effect|
216    ec0, undo = ec0.apply(effect)
217    undos[key] = undo
218    final = ec0
219  end
220  undos.keys.permutation do |order|
221    ec = order.reduce(final) { |acc, key| undos[key].call(acc) }
222    assert_equal GAMMA0, ec.state
223    assert ec.sound?(GAMMA0)
224  end
225end

192check "Theorem 73 (dynamic history leaves no trace): load–unload–reload ≈ statically assembled" do
193  # Take a system through a swap and back down to the target composition…
194  eventful = machine_with_tree(seed: 4).run
195  eventful.o_retire("logger")
196  eventful.run
197  eventful.o_remove("logger")
198  eventful.o_insert("logger", logger("2.0"))
199  eventful.run

201  fresh = Cordis::Calculus::Machine.new(seed: 9)
202  fresh.o_insert("dash", dashboard)
203  fresh.o_insert("metrics", metrics)
204  fresh.o_insert("logger", logger("2.0"))
205  fresh.o_insert("config", config_provider)
206  fresh.run
207  assert_equal fresh.snapshot, eventful.snapshot
208end
